People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten… weird. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. I mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.

Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.

And then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.

So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten… weird. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Some days I’ll skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal because cooking for one feels kind of pointless.\n\nI mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. That’s part of what bothered me, actually. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. I don’t think so… I mean, normal weight is such a funny phrase, because I know what it means medically, but I don’t really track it that way. At work we use numbers all the time for other people, and then when it’s me I sort of avoid looking too closely.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really meaning to and my weight drops, and then other nights I’ll end up picking at cereal or crackers because cooking for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. It’s more… I don’t know, I just don’t always want to deal with food.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you ever worried about gaining weight, regardless of your current weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / healthcare_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… I keep coming back to that, don’t I? I mean, I can worry about plenty of things—my car making that weird noise, whether I answered an email, whether I forgot to buy coffee—but weight isn’t usually the thing my mind grabs onto.

If I avoid eating, it’s not because I’m thinking, “Oh no, I’ll gain weight.” It’s more like I just don’t have the energy to make a decision about food. But no, I haven’t noticed myself being worried about gaining weight regardless of what I weigh.

Some days I’ll skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal because cooking for one feels kind of pointless.\n\nI mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. That’s part of what bothered me, actually. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I think it’s more that I’m not interested in food, or maybe not interested in dealing with it. Like, I’ll get home and dinner just feels like this whole task, and cooking for just me feels kind of pointless, so I skip it without really deciding to.

I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. I think it’s tied to my mood, mostly. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m making some big decision not to eat, but when I’m feeling low, food just becomes another chore.

That’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite has been all over the place for months. I’ll have stretches where I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m just standing there picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for one person feels… I don’t know, pointless. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten… weird. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Some days I’ll skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal because cooking for one feels kind of pointless.\n\nI mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. That’s part of what bothered me, actually. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really meaning to and my weight drops, and then other nights I’ll end up picking at cereal or crackers because cooking for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Like, I’ll lose weight without trying, and then I guess some of it comes back when I’m eating more randomly again. It’s not very organized.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight even when you're at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, normal weight is such a funny phrase, because I know what it means medically, but I don’t really track it that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the process, I think. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.

What finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.

Which sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. But lately it’s just… food is there, I’m just not going through the effort of eating properly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten… weird. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Some days I’ll skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal because cooking for one feels kind of pointless.\n\nI mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. That’s part of what bothered me, actually. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really meaning to and my weight drops, and then other nights I’ll end up picking at cereal or crackers because cooking for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Like, I’ll lose weight without trying, and then I guess some of it comes back when I’m eating more randomly again. It’s not very organized.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight even when you're at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, normal weight is such a funny phrase, because I know what it means medically, but I don’t really track it that way. At work we use numbers all the time for other people, and then when it’s me I sort of avoid looking too closely.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight. It’s not like I look at the scale and panic or feel like I need to stop eating. It’s more… I don’t know, I just don’t always want to deal with food.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about gaining weight, regardless of your current weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep coming back to that, don’t I? I mean, I can worry about plenty of things—my car making that weird noise, whether I answered an email, whether I forgot to buy coffee—but weight isn’t usually the thing my mind grabs onto.\n\nIf I avoid eating, it’s not because I’m thinking, “Oh no, I’ll gain weight.” It’s more like I just don’t have the energy to make a decision about food. But no, I haven’t noticed myself being worried about gaining weight regardless of what I weigh.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating more because you're not interested in food or because of some other reason?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not interested in food, or maybe not interested in dealing with it. Like, I’ll get home and dinner just feels like this whole task, and cooking for just me feels kind of pointless, so I skip it without really deciding to.\n\nThen other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and eat crackers or cereal, which is ridiculous because I know better—I talk to people about routines all the time at work—but when it’s me, it’s just… whatever is easiest. That up-and-down appetite and losing weight without meaning to is part of why I finally came in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in food related to your mood or is it a separate issue?\n\nPatient: I think it’s tied to my mood, mostly. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m making some big decision not to eat, but when I’m feeling low, food just becomes another chore.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite has been all over the place for months. I’ll have stretches where I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m just standing there picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for one person feels… I don’t know, pointless. Like too much effort for something I don’t even care about.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about food itself that makes you not want to eat, or is it more about not feeling like going through the process of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s more the process, I think. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.\n\nWhich sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really meaning to and my weight drops, and then other nights I’ll end up picking at cereal or crackers because cooking for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Like, I’ll lose weight without trying, and then I guess some of it comes back when I’m eating more randomly again. It’s not very organized.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight even when you're at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, normal weight is such a funny phrase, because I know what it means medically, but I don’t really track it that way. At work we use numbers all the time for other people, and then when it’s me I sort of avoid looking too closely.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight. It’s not like I look at the scale and panic or feel like I need to stop eating. It’s more… I don’t know, I just don’t always want to deal with food.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about gaining weight, regardless of your current weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep coming back to that, don’t I? I mean, I can worry about plenty of things—my car making that weird noise, whether I answered an email, whether I forgot to buy coffee—but weight isn’t usually the thing my mind grabs onto.\n\nIf I avoid eating, it’s not because I’m thinking, “Oh no, I’ll gain weight.” It’s more like I just don’t have the energy to make a decision about food. But no, I haven’t noticed myself being worried about gaining weight regardless of what I weigh.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating more because you're not interested in food or because of some other reason?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not interested in food, or maybe not interested in dealing with it. Like, I’ll get home and dinner just feels like this whole task, and cooking for just me feels kind of pointless, so I skip it without really deciding to.\n\nThen other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and eat crackers or cereal, which is ridiculous because I know better—I talk to people about routines all the time at work—but when it’s me, it’s just… whatever is easiest. That up-and-down appetite and losing weight without meaning to is part of why I finally came in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in food related to your mood or is it a separate issue?\n\nPatient: I think it’s tied to my mood, mostly. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m making some big decision not to eat, but when I’m feeling low, food just becomes another chore.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite has been all over the place for months. I’ll have stretches where I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m just standing there picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for one person feels… I don’t know, pointless. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.\n\nWhich sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. But lately it’s just… food is there, I’m just not going through the effort of eating properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what or how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal?\n\nPatient: Not exactly out of control, no. It’s not like I’m binging or feeling like I can’t stop. It’s more… I just don’t have the energy or interest to make an actual meal, so I end up eating whatever is sitting there.\n\nLike, I’ll stand there with cereal or crackers and sort of pick at it, and then that becomes dinner, which is embarrassing because I do know better. But cooking for myself just feels pointless lately. And then I go through stretches where I skip dinner altogether and notice I’m losing weight without meaning to. That’s part of why I finally thought, okay, this has been going on for months and I probably need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating pattern more because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you are simply not interested in eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really about being afraid of gaining weight. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is there something specific about food itself that makes you not want to eat, or is it more about not feeling like going through the process of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s more the process, I think. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.\n\nWhich sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. But lately it’s just… food is there, I’m just not going through the effort of eating properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what or how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal?\n\nPatient: Not exactly out of control, no. It’s not like I’m binging or feeling like I can’t stop. It’s more… I just don’t have the energy or interest to make an actual meal, so I end up eating whatever is sitting there.\n\nLike, I’ll stand there with cereal or crackers and sort of pick at it, and then that becomes dinner, which is embarrassing because I do know better. But cooking for myself just feels pointless lately. And then I go through stretches where I skip dinner altogether and notice I’m losing weight without meaning to. That’s part of why I finally thought, okay, this has been going on for months and I probably need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating pattern more because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you are simply not interested in eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really about being afraid of gaining weight. I mean, I notice when my weight changes, but I’m not trying to make that happen.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just not interested, or I can’t get myself to care enough to make food. And then I’ll realize I skipped dinner again, or I’ve been sort of grazing on crackers or cereal instead of eating an actual meal. Cooking for just me starts to feel pointless, which is strange because I used to be pretty good about meals. It’s been going on for months, and that’s part of why I finally came in.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your lack of interest in eating related to a fear of gaining weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / healthcare_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What finally made me come in is that my appetite has been all over the place for months, with stretches where I skip dinner and lose weight without trying and other nights when I pick at crackers or cereal just because cooking for myself feels pointless.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. It’s not a fear of gaining weight.

It’s more that my appetite has just been all over the place for months. Some nights I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’ll just pick at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels… I don’t know, pointless. I used to be better about making real meals, but lately I just don’t seem to care enough to do it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten… weird. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Some days I’ll skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’m just standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal because cooking for one feels kind of pointless.\n\nI mean, I’m a social worker, so I know I’d tell someone else not to ignore that, but it’s different when it’s you, you know? I kept thinking it was just a phase or being busy, but it hasn’t really settled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been trying to lose weight. That’s part of what bothered me, actually. It’s more like I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really deciding to, and then I notice my clothes feel a little looser.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m not exactly hungry, but I’ll stand in the kitchen picking at crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, like too much trouble. It’s been going on for months, kind of up and down.\n\nDoctor: How much weight would you say you've lost over these months?\n\nPatient: I don’t have an exact number, honestly. I’ve noticed my clothes are looser, and it seems to happen during those stretches where I’m just not eating dinner much. I keep meaning to actually weigh myself regularly, but then I get distracted or I’m rushing in the morning… and with my work, I’m always telling clients to track things, which is ironic.\n\nBut no, I wasn’t trying to lose any weight. It’s just been this on-and-off thing over the past few months where my appetite kind of disappears, and then other nights I’m just grazing instead of making a meal.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood or emotional state affects whether you eat or skip meals?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does, though it’s hard to separate out. It’s not like I’m consciously saying, “I’m upset, so I won’t eat.” It’s more that when I’m feeling low or just sort of… flat, dinner feels like this whole production, especially just for me. So I’ll skip it, and then after a while I notice I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I’m wandering around the kitchen eating crackers or cereal, not really making a meal. Which is funny because I’ll talk with clients about routines and taking care of themselves, and then I come home and can’t be bothered to boil pasta. So yes, I guess my mood does affect it, but in this indirect, messy way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had the usual “oh, these pants are tight” thoughts like anybody, but gaining weight isn’t what’s been on my mind.\n\nHonestly, what pushed me to come in was more the opposite, or just how erratic it’s gotten. For months my appetite has been all over the place—some nights I skip dinner without really meaning to and then I lose a little weight, and other nights I’m standing there eating crackers or cereal because cooking just for myself feels kind of pointless. It’s embarrassing, actually, because I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel guilty after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not exactly guilty after eating. More annoyed with myself if I’ve done that thing where I eat random snacks instead of an actual meal, but that’s less about the food and more like, “Really? This is what you’re calling dinner?” \n\nI do that with paperwork too—put it off and then sort of patch it together later. But no, I wouldn’t say I feel guilty in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: Not really in that “large amount in an hour” kind of way, no. It’s more scattered than that. Like, I’ll skip dinner for a few nights here and there without exactly deciding to, and then some other night I’m sort of grazing on crackers or cereal because making a real meal for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nIt’s been going on for months, this all-over-the-place appetite thing, and I did lose some weight without trying. But I wouldn’t call it a big binge or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I mean, I’ve felt nauseated once in a while like anyone, especially if something doesn’t sit right, but I’ve never made myself throw up after eating.\n\nI had a roommate years ago who struggled with that, actually, and I remember how secretive and exhausting it seemed for her—but no, that’s not something I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to try to burn off calories?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I walk sometimes, but that’s more to get out of the house or because parking near the hospital is ridiculous—well, near any medical building, really, it’s always some maze.\n\nBut no, I’m not exercising to burn off calories. I’m definitely not doing anything excessive like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this erratic eating pattern caused you any significant problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still getting to work, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing. And I am, technically. But my notes and documentation pile up in a way they didn’t used to, and then I’m staying late to catch up on things that used to feel pretty routine.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s more obvious, I guess. I’ve let a lot of friendships sort of… fade into the background. Even basic routines have shrunk down to almost nothing. I tell myself I’ll text someone back or make a normal dinner or do laundry at a normal time, and then it just doesn’t happen.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t think that’s what’s driving it. I’m not sitting there worried about gaining weight in some intense way.\n\nIt’s more like food just becomes this whole… thing, and then I get distracted by something else, or I don’t feel like dealing with it. Though, funny enough, I used to love cooking when my kitchen was organized—there was this one soup I’d make all the time—but no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you gained back the weight you lost?\n\nPatient: A little, I think? It’s been kind of up and down more than one clear pattern. I’ll go through stretches where I just skip dinner without really meaning to and my weight drops, and then other nights I’ll end up picking at cereal or crackers because cooking for just me feels… I don’t know, pointless.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite’s been all over the place for months. Like, I’ll lose weight without trying, and then I guess some of it comes back when I’m eating more randomly again. It’s not very organized.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight even when you're at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, normal weight is such a funny phrase, because I know what it means medically, but I don’t really track it that way. At work we use numbers all the time for other people, and then when it’s me I sort of avoid looking too closely.\n\nBut no, I wouldn’t say I’m afraid of gaining weight. It’s not like I look at the scale and panic or feel like I need to stop eating. It’s more… I don’t know, I just don’t always want to deal with food.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about gaining weight, regardless of your current weight?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep coming back to that, don’t I? I mean, I can worry about plenty of things—my car making that weird noise, whether I answered an email, whether I forgot to buy coffee—but weight isn’t usually the thing my mind grabs onto.\n\nIf I avoid eating, it’s not because I’m thinking, “Oh no, I’ll gain weight.” It’s more like I just don’t have the energy to make a decision about food. But no, I haven’t noticed myself being worried about gaining weight regardless of what I weigh.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating more because you're not interested in food or because of some other reason?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not interested in food, or maybe not interested in dealing with it. Like, I’ll get home and dinner just feels like this whole task, and cooking for just me feels kind of pointless, so I skip it without really deciding to.\n\nThen other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and eat crackers or cereal, which is ridiculous because I know better—I talk to people about routines all the time at work—but when it’s me, it’s just… whatever is easiest. That up-and-down appetite and losing weight without meaning to is part of why I finally came in, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in food related to your mood or is it a separate issue?\n\nPatient: I think it’s tied to my mood, mostly. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m making some big decision not to eat, but when I’m feeling low, food just becomes another chore.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite has been all over the place for months. I’ll have stretches where I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m just standing there picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for one person feels… I don’t know, pointless. Like too much effort for something I don’t even care about.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about food itself that makes you not want to eat, or is it more about not feeling like going through the process of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s more the process, I think. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.\n\nWhich sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. But lately it’s just… food is there, I’m just not going through the effort of eating properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what or how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal?\n\nPatient: Not exactly out of control, no. It’s not like I’m binging or feeling like I can’t stop. It’s more… I just don’t have the energy or interest to make an actual meal, so I end up eating whatever is sitting there.\n\nLike, I’ll stand there with cereal or crackers and sort of pick at it, and then that becomes dinner, which is embarrassing because I do know better. But cooking for myself just feels pointless lately. And then I go through stretches where I skip dinner altogether and notice I’m losing weight without meaning to. That’s part of why I finally thought, okay, this has been going on for months and I probably need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating pattern more because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you are simply not interested in eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really about being afraid of gaining weight. I mean, I notice when my weight changes, but I’m not trying to make that happen.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just not interested, or I can’t get myself to care enough to make food. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not in a dramatic way, like I’m making some big decision not to eat, but when I’m feeling low, food just becomes another chore.\n\nThat’s actually part of what finally made me come in. My appetite has been all over the place for months. I’ll have stretches where I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m just standing there picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for one person feels… I don’t know, pointless. Like too much effort for something I don’t even care about.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about food itself that makes you not want to eat, or is it more about not feeling like going through the process of eating?\n\nPatient: It’s more the process, I think. There isn’t anything about food itself that disgusts me or makes me afraid to eat it. It’s more like… by the time I’m supposed to make dinner, it just feels like one more thing I can’t quite get myself to do.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in was that this has been going on for months. I’ll have stretches where I just skip dinner and then notice I’ve lost weight without trying, and then other nights I’m not really eating a meal, just picking at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels pointless.\n\nWhich sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I used to make decent meals, and I know how to take care of myself in theory. But lately it’s just… food is there, I’m just not going through the effort of eating properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what or how much you eat when you're standing in the kitchen eating crackers or cereal?\n\nPatient: Not exactly out of control, no. It’s not like I’m binging or feeling like I can’t stop. It’s more… I just don’t have the energy or interest to make an actual meal, so I end up eating whatever is sitting there.\n\nLike, I’ll stand there with cereal or crackers and sort of pick at it, and then that becomes dinner, which is embarrassing because I do know better. But cooking for myself just feels pointless lately. And then I go through stretches where I skip dinner altogether and notice I’m losing weight without meaning to. That’s part of why I finally thought, okay, this has been going on for months and I probably need to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your erratic eating pattern more because you are afraid of gaining weight or because you are simply not interested in eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really about being afraid of gaining weight. I mean, I notice when my weight changes, but I’m not trying to make that happen.\n\nIt’s more that I’m just not interested, or I can’t get myself to care enough to make food. And then I’ll realize I skipped dinner again, or I’ve been sort of grazing on crackers or cereal instead of eating an actual meal. Cooking for just me starts to feel pointless, which is strange because I used to be pretty good about meals. It’s been going on for months, and that’s part of why I finally came in.\n\nDoctor: Is your lack of interest in eating related to a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It’s not a fear of gaining weight.\n\nIt’s more that my appetite has just been all over the place for months. Some nights I skip dinner and then realize I’ve lost weight without trying, and other nights I’ll just pick at crackers or cereal because cooking for myself feels… I don’t know, pointless. I used to be better about making real meals, but lately I just don’t seem to care enough to do it."
